⏺ Here's what the filter does to the 5,000:

  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┬───────────────┐
  │                                                         │     Count     │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────┤
  │ User prompts                                            │ 9,550         │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────┤
  │ Kept (32–1,536 characters)                              │ 7,135 (74.7%) │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────┤
  │ Removed as too short (greetings, "continue", …)         │ 2,098         │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────┤
  │ Removed as too long (pasted documents)                  │ 317           │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────┤
  │ Conversations with at least 1 kept prompt               │ 4,299         │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────┤
  │ Conversations with none, so they drop out of clustering │ 701 (14%)     │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┴───────────────┘

  Plan: two clusterings from the same embeddings, both using all-mpnet-base-v2
  and 20 clusters:

  ┌───────────┬──────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────┐
  │           │     A. Prompt-level      │      B. Conversation-level       │
  │           │  (replicates the paper)  │                                  │
  ├───────────┼──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │           │ each kept user prompt    │ each conversation: the average   │
  │ Unit      │ (7,135)                  │ of its kept prompts' embeddings  │
  │           │                          │ (4,299)                          │
  ├───────────┼──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Compared  │ the paper's 20 clusters  │ human and LLM labels, one per    │
  │ with      │ and 5 categories, on the │ conversation                     │
  │           │  same unit               │                                  │
  └───────────┴──────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────┘

  Comparing A with B shows directly how much the choice of unit (conversation or
  individual prompt) changes the picture.

  Where we need to be careful:
  1. The 701 dropped conversations aren't random. Compared with the full sample
     they contain more Others (143, which are short greetings) and more Language
     (223, many of them long pasted documents to summarize). So every
     conversation-level comparison has to use the same 4,299 conversations. I'd
     recompute the LLM and human percentages on that subset rather than reuse
     the 5,000 figures.
  2. The random 60 shrinks to 52 for comparisons involving clustering (8 drop
     out). The 100 becomes 90.
  3. A fair bridge to the paper: for prompt-level comparisons, I'd also count
     each conversation's LLM label once per kept prompt. That gives an LLM
     distribution on the paper's unit, and should explain part of the Explicit
     gap (role-play conversations have many prompts).
  4. Settings the paper doesn't specify: I'd normalize the embeddings (the model
     is built for cosine similarity) and run k-means with a fixed seed of 42
     and 10 restarts, and document that.
  5. Naming the clusters, as the paper did: send the 100 prompts closest to each
     cluster's center to the model in OPENAI_MODEL for a short topic summary.
     Then you map the 20 clusters to the 6 categories yourself, before looking
     at any agreement numbers.

  One cost: sentence-transformers brings in PyTorch, a download of a few hundred
  MB. Embedding about 7K prompts on your Mac takes a few minutes.

❯ interesting, so what will happen is we will replicate the paper exactly,
  using the prompts only and also at conversation level. I want to understand
  this first

⏺ Yes, that's the idea. Here's how the two versions differ and what each
  comparison tells you.

  What "replicating the paper" means here

  We repeat the paper's steps on our data:
  1. Take the user prompts, including follow-ups.
  2. Drop prompts under 32 or over 1,536 characters.
  3. Embed each prompt with all-mpnet-base-v2.
  4. Group them with k-means into 20 clusters.
  5. Name each cluster from the 100 prompts closest to its center.

  It won't reproduce the paper's exact numbers:
  - We have about 7K prompts from 5,000 conversations; the paper had prompts
    from 100K conversations.
  - k-means gives somewhat different clusters on different data.
  - The paper named clusters with GPT-4; we'll use your model.
  - A few settings aren't given in the paper.

  So this replicates the method. If our category percentages come out close to
  the paper's, that's evidence the method gives stable results, not just a copy
  of their figures.

  The two versions

  A. Prompt-level (the paper's way): every kept prompt is its own point. A
  conversation with 5 prompts contributes 5 points, and they can land in
  different clusters. Percentages are shares of prompts.

  B. Conversation-level (our way): each conversation becomes one point, the
  average of its prompts' embeddings, and gets exactly one cluster. Percentages
  are shares of conversations, the same unit as your labels and the LLM's.

  Why the unit changes the result: a small example

  ┌───────────────────────────────────┬─────────────┬──────────────────────┐
  │           Conversation            │    User     │   Kept after the     │
  │                                   │   prompts   │        filter        │
  ├───────────────────────────────────┼─────────────┼──────────────────────┤
  │ 1. Coding question                │ 1           │ 1                    │
  ├───────────────────────────────────┼─────────────┼──────────────────────┤
  │ 2. Erotic role-play               │ 10          │ 10                   │
  ├───────────────────────────────────┼─────────────┼──────────────────────┤
  │ 3. "hi" then a translation        │ 2           │ 1 ("hi" is too       │
  │ request                           │             │ short)               │
  └───────────────────────────────────┴─────────────┴──────────────────────┘

  - Prompt-level: 12 points, 10 of them explicit, so Explicit = 83%.
  - Conversation-level: 3 points, 1 explicit, so Explicit = 33%.

  Same data, very different answer. That's the likely reason our LLM's Explicit
  share (6.4%) is below the paper's (9.6%): long role-play conversations count
  many times when counting prompts.

  A useful property of averaging: if 4 of a conversation's 5 prompts are about
  coding, the average sits close to coding. That roughly matches your labeling
  rule, "the theme most user messages are about." The weakness is a conversation
  split evenly between two topics: its average falls between them and may land
  in a cluster that fits neither.

  What we can compare

  ┌───────────────────┬─────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────┐
  │    Comparison     │        Unit         │     Question it answers      │
  ├───────────────────┼─────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │                   │                     │ Does the paper's method give │
  │ Paper vs A        │ prompts             │  the same category shares on │
  │                   │                     │  our sample?                 │
  ├───────────────────┼─────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ A vs B            │ prompts vs          │ How much does the unit       │
  │                   │ conversations       │ change the result?           │
  ├───────────────────┼─────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │                   │ conversations       │ Do clusters agree with the   │
  │ B vs LLM          │ (4,299)             │ LLM, conversation by         │
  │                   │                     │ conversation?                │
  ├───────────────────┼─────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ B vs human        │ conversations (52   │ Do clusters agree with you?  │
  │                   │ random, 90 of 100)  │                              │
  ├───────────────────┼─────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ LLM vs human      │ conversations (100) │ Done already: kappa 0.70 for │
  │                   │                     │  category                    │
  ├───────────────────┼─────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ LLM counted per   │                     │ The fair bridge: the LLM's   │
  │ prompt vs paper   │ prompts             │ labels counted on the        │
  │ and A             │                     │ paper's unit                 │
  └───────────────────┴─────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────┘

  What you'll do

  Both A and B produce 20 named clusters. You map each cluster to one of the 6
  categories, once for A and once for B, before seeing any comparison. Every
  cluster result then becomes category percentages that sit directly next to the
  human, LLM and paper columns.

  Does this match what you had in mind? If so, I'll add sentence-transformers
  and write the clustering script.

❯ question: are we doing the 32 to 1536 filter for both prompts and
  conversations ?

⏺ Yes, as planned, but the filter works on prompts, not on conversations as a
  whole:

  - A (prompt-level): each prompt is kept or dropped by its own length (32–1,536
    characters).
  - B (conversation-level): built from the same kept prompts. A conversation's
    vector is the average of its kept prompts only. If none of its prompts pass,
    the conversation has no vector and drops out. That's the 701.

  So in B the filter acts indirectly: short and very long prompts are left out
  of the average. Nobody measures the length of the whole conversation.

  Why use the same filter for both: it makes A vs B a clean comparison. The
  prompts and the embeddings are identical, and the only thing that changes is
  the unit (prompt or conversation). Any difference between A and B then comes
  from the unit alone.

  The alternative, no filter for B: every conversation gets a vector, so B
  covers all 5,000 and lines up with the full LLM labels. Two downsides:
  - "hi", "continue" and "thanks" get averaged in and pull conversations toward
    a vague greeting area.
  - Pasted documents over ~380 tokens are silently cut off by the model anyway,
    so the "no filter" version still isn't complete.

  I'd use the same filter for both, and handle the 701 separately: report how
  many there are and what the LLM labeled them (mostly Others and Language).
  Then every comparison involving B uses those same 4,299 conversations.

❯ but 1536 is reasonable for a message but not for a conversation.
  conversations are long

⏺ Right, and that's why the 1,536 limit never applies to a conversation's total
  length. It applies to each user message separately.

  Take a conversation with 30 user messages of 500 characters each. That's
  15,000 characters in total, yet every message passes the filter, so all 30 are
  embedded and averaged. The conversation is kept in full, however long it is.
  The longest conversation we keep has 45 kept prompts.

  What the filter removes inside a conversation is only:
  - individual messages under 32 characters, such as "hi", "continue" or "ok"
    (2,098 messages)
  - individual messages over 1,536 characters, usually a pasted document or a
    long block of code (317 messages)

  A conversation drops out completely only if every one of its user messages
  falls outside that range: for example, a conversation that is just "hi", or a
  single pasted document. That's the 701.

  There's also no reason to cap conversation length for B. The model never reads
  the whole conversation at once. It reads one message at a time, which suits
  the 1,536-character limit, and the averaging step combines the messages. So B
  represents long conversations completely.

  The one real loss is the 317 long messages. If a conversation's main request
  is a pasted document ("summarize this: …"), B never sees it. If you want B to
  keep them, an option is to split long messages into chunks of up to ~1,536
  characters, embed each chunk, and average them too. That differs from the
  paper, though, so I'd use it only for B and keep A exactly as the paper did.
  My recommendation is to keep the paper's filter for both, so the only
  difference between A and B is the unit.

❯ lets keep the filter on individual messages for both. NOT at conversation
  level. Does that make sense ?

⏺ Yes, that's the right setup, and it's exactly what I'll build:

  - The filter (32–1,536 characters) applies to each user message, in both A and
    B.
  - There's no filter on conversation length. In B, a conversation of any length
    is kept and represented by the average of all its kept messages.
  - A and B use the same kept messages and the same embeddings. The only
    difference is whether each message or each conversation is counted as one
    item.

  The one consequence: a conversation where every user message fails the filter
  (about 701, for example "hi"-only chats or a single pasted document) has
  nothing to embed, so it can't appear in B. That's a side effect of the message
  filter, not a conversation filter, and we'll report it.

  Should I go ahead: add sentence-transformers and write the clustering script?

  Differences worth noting:
  - Explicit is one cluster for us, not two. At 5.8% it's below the paper's
    9.6%, which fits the LLM's 6.4%. A smaller sample likely merges the paper's
    two explicit clusters into one.
  - New groups the paper didn't name:
    - math and logic word problems (A10, B5)
    - "classifying behaviors as acceptable or wrong" (A6), probably a scripted
      template
    - jailbreak prompts (A13, B20)
  - Scripted templates get bigger at the conversation level. For example, the
    chemical clusters go from 4.5% of prompts to 7.4% of conversations, because
    those scripts are single-prompt conversations.

  Where things are (in analysis/clustering/):
  - clusters_A.md and clusters_B.md: for mapping. Each cluster has its title, a
    one-line description and the 15 prompts closest to its center.
  - clusters_A.csv and clusters_B.csv: size, percentage, title and description
    for each cluster.
  - prompts_A.csv and conversations_B.csv: the cluster assigned to each prompt
    and each conversation.
  - The embeddings are cached in data/embeddings/, which git ignores, and can be
    rebuilt.

  Settings the paper doesn't give, which I chose: normalized embeddings, k-means
  with seed 42 and 10 restarts, clusters numbered by size, and cluster names
  from OPENAI_MODEL instead of GPT-4.

❯ Initially I had planned that I will manually map the clusters to category -
  is that right ?

⏺ Yes. Earlier you said you would map the clusters to categories yourself, first
  planned for 10 clusters and now 20. It's also the right approach, for three
  reasons:

  1. It's what the paper did. The paper's 5 categories (the colors in Figure 3)
     are the authors' grouping of their 20 clusters by hand. Mapping by hand
     repeats that step.
  2. It keeps the clustering independent. You could map automatically by giving
     each cluster the most common LLM label among its conversations, but then
     "clusters vs LLM" agreement would be built in, and the comparison would
     mean nothing.
  3. You already know the categories best. You wrote the definitions and labeled
     100 conversations with them.

  To keep the mapping fair:
  - Map before seeing any comparison numbers, using only the cluster titles and
    the example prompts in the .md files.
  - One category per cluster. For a mixed cluster, pick the category most of its
    examples fit. Mixed clusters are a real limitation of clustering, and
    they'll show up as disagreement.
  - Map A and B separately. They're different clusterings with different
    numbering.
  - Note a reason for unclear cases, for example A6 ("classifying behaviors as
    acceptable or wrong") or A13 (jailbreaks), which have no obvious home. The
    notes help in the write-up.

  Should I create the mapping.yaml template with all 40 clusters, their titles
  and an empty category, for you to fill in?

  Others, so this cluster is a bit mixed. Clustering grouped it by the shared
  template, not by the task.

  now under Technical, the clustering treats that template the way the LLM did.

  #: 1

  What it compares: Cluster A vs cluster B vs the paper. Topic-by-topic match
    (chemical, plant, toxic, explicit…) plus shares by category

  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 2

    with and without Others

  #: 3

  What it compares: human vs LLM, LLM vs B, human vs B: % agreement, kappa,
    confusion matrices

  ────────────────────────────────────────

    ranges

  #: 6

  What it compares: prevented or failed per model, among conversations where
    it applies
  Unit and subset: 695 applicable; rank models with ≥20

  Other reports worth considering

  #: 0
  Report: Method: the paper vs us
  Why it's useful: A table of every step (sample, unit, filter, embedding, k,
    naming, categories) and what we did. Every other result depends on it.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 7

  Why it's useful: Do our "harmful" and "unsafe" labels match the dataset's
    moderation flags? What do the flags miss (sycophancy, anthropomorphism,
    refusals)? This connects to your earlier observation about moderation.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 8

  Why it's useful: Which topics carry which traits, e.g. anthropomorphism in
    role-play, harmful in Cultural, failed prevention in Explicit.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 9
  Report: Single vs multi-turn
  Why it's useful: Do longer conversations fail prevention more often (gradual
    jailbreaks)? We already saw flag rates climb from 4% to 23% with length.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 10
  Report: Where human and LLM disagree
  Why it's useful: A few real examples with the LLM's reasoning, e.g. "happy
    to help" and the text-completion template. This explains the weak kappas
    honestly.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 11
  Report: Coverage
  Why it's useful: The 701 conversations clustering can't include, and what
    they are. A short note, which could also go inside report 0.

  1. Method vs the paper (0): the context for everything else.

        3 +# LMSYS-Chat-1M: topics, model behaviour and safety in 5,000 E
          +nglish conversations                                          
        4 +                                                              
        5 +This study revisits the topic analysis of the LMSYS-Chat-1M pa
          +per ([Zheng et al., 2023](https://arxiv.org/html/2309.11998v4)
          +) and extends it to model behaviour. On a random sample of 5,0
          +00 English conversations we:                                  
        6 +                                                              
        7 +1. labeled every conversation with **6 categories** (the paper
          +'s 5 plus *Others*), **4 attributes** of the model's replies (
          +anthropomorphism, sycophancy, fear-inducing, harmful) and whet
          +her the model **prevented unsafe content**, using one set of w
          +ritten guidelines for both a human annotator and an LLM;      
        8 +2. **replicated the paper's k-means clustering** (20 clusters)
          + twice: once per prompt, as the paper did, and once per conver
          +sation;                                                       
        9 +3. compared **human vs LLM vs clustering vs the paper**, and l
          +ooked at which chatbot models show which behaviours.          
       10 +                                                              
       11 +**Headlines**                                                 
       12 +                                                              
       13 +- The LLM labels the categories like the human annotator does 
          +(κ = 0.70, 76% agreement); k-means clusters agree only moderat
          +ely with either (κ = 0.47 vs human, 0.45 vs LLM).             
       14 +- Our category shares are close to the paper's except *Busines
          +s & Specific* (21.4% vs 15.8%), because our guidelines send ge
          +neral-knowledge questions there.                              
       15 +- Counting per prompt instead of per conversation raises *Expl
          +icit Content* from 6.5% to 8.4% (paper: 9.6%): explicit role-p
          +lay conversations are longer.                                 
       16 +- Unsafe content was requested or produced in 13.9% of convers
          +ations; models prevented it only **54.4%** of the time, rangin
          +g from llama-2-13b-chat (100.0%), oasst-pythia-12b (81.1%), ch
          +atglm-6b (77.1%) down to vicuna-33b (17.6%), alpaca-13b (45.9%
          +), vicuna-13b (49.4%).                                        
       17 +- OpenAI's moderation flags miss 57% of the conversations our 
          +labels mark as unsafe, and 85% of those with anthropomorphism,
          + sycophancy or fear-inducing replies.                         
       18 +                                                              
       19 +---                                                           
       20 +                                                              
       21 +## 1. Data and method, compared with the paper                
       22 +                                                              
       23 +| Step | Paper (Sec. 3.2) | This study |                      
       24 +|---|---|---|                                                 
       25 +| Sample | 100K random English conversations | 5,000 random En
          +glish conversations (seed 42), 25 models |                    
       26 +| Unit for topics | every user prompt (initial + follow-ups) |
          + prompts (clustering A) **and** conversations (clustering B, h
          +uman, LLM) |                                                  
       27 +| Length filter | drop prompts < 32 or > 1,536 characters | sa
          +me, applied to each message (never to a whole conversation) | 
       28 +| Deduplication | none | none |

⏺ Written for: readers of the GitHub repository, researchers who know the
  LMSYS-Chat-1M paper but not this project.

  The report is ready in REPORT.md, with 6 matplotlib charts in
  reports/figures/. I looked at each chart for layout problems and fixed the one
  label overlap. I also checked the claims in the text against the data, and
  corrected two that the data didn't support.

  Every number is generated, not typed. scripts/make_reports.py computes all
  values, tables and charts from the committed CSVs and fills them into
  reports/REPORT.template.md. To change the wording, edit the template and rerun
  uv run python scripts/make_reports.py. Don't edit REPORT.md directly; it gets
  overwritten.

  What's in it:
  - Introduction and headlines: what we did and the five main findings.
  - 1. Method vs the paper: a step-by-step table, who labeled what, and the
    4,299-conversation subset.
  - 2. Prompt vs conversation: the effect of the unit, and the paper's topics
    reappearing.
  - 3. Categories: human vs LLM vs clustering vs paper, with agreement and
    confusion matrices.
  - 4. Attributes: how common they are, agreement, by category and by model.
  - 5. Unsafe-content prevention: by model with error ranges, sexual vs other,
    single vs multi-turn.
  - 6. Our labels vs OpenAI moderation
  - 7. Limitations
  - Appendices: both cluster lists, the 701 conversations clustering can't
    cover, and reproduction commands.

  Main findings:
  - Human vs LLM categories agree substantially (κ 0.70). Clustering agrees only
    moderately with either (κ 0.45–0.47), because k-means groups prompts by
    wording and templates.
  - Counting per prompt vs per conversation: with the same LLM labels, Explicit
    Content rises from 6.5% to 8.4%. The paper's figure is 9.6%.
  - Models prevented unsafe content only 54% of the time.
    - Clearly better than average: llama-2-13b-chat, oasst-pythia-12b,
      chatglm-6b, llama-13b.
    - Clearly worse: vicuna-33b, at 18%.
    - Sexual requests were prevented far less often than other unsafe requests
      (38% vs 65%).
  - OpenAI moderation doesn't flag 57% of what we label unsafe, and 85% of
    conversations with anthropomorphism, sycophancy or fear-inducing replies.
  - Caveats shown in the report:
    - Anthropomorphism is unreliable (κ 0.24), because the guideline's "happy to
      help" example made the LLM count stock politeness.
    - The commercial models (GPT-4, Claude) had too few unsafe requests to rate.

  Not committed: REPORT.md, reports/ and scripts/make_reports.py. Should I
  commit and push them?

❯ did we also make LLM category per prompt ?

⏺ No. The "LLM per prompt" column isn't a separate per-prompt labeling. Every
  prompt inherits its conversation's LLM label, so a conversation's label is
  counted once for each of its kept prompts. The report's footnote says this
  ("counts each conversation's LLM label once per kept prompt"), but the column
  name "LLM per prompt" can easily be read as more than that.

  What it measures and what it doesn't:
  - It shows how weighting by number of prompts shifts the shares. That's the
    pure unit effect, and the source of the Explicit change from 6.5% to 8.4%.
  - It doesn't capture topic changes inside a conversation. In a 5-prompt
    conversation that's mostly coding with one translation request, all 5
    prompts count as Technical. A true per-prompt label would count 4 Technical
    and 1 Language.
  - Since 69% of conversations are single-turn and most multi-turn ones stay on
    one theme, the difference is probably small, but we haven't measured it.

  Two options:
  1. Keep it and rename it to something like "LLM, weighted per prompt", with a
     clear note that it's the conversation label counted once per prompt. No
     extra cost.
  2. Actually label each prompt with the LLM: 7,135 kept prompts, each labeled
     for category only and without the rest of the conversation. That matches
     what the paper's k-means saw, and makes LLM-per-prompt vs clustering A a
     true like-for-like comparison. The catch is that follow-ups like "make it
     shorter" or "continue the story" are ambiguous on their own, exactly as
     they were for the paper. It costs about as much as the 5,000 run and needs
     a small addition to llm_annotate.py (a prompt-level mode with category
     only).

  Option 2 would let the report separate the weighting effect from the effect of
  judging prompts without context. If you don't need that, option 1 is honest
  and enough. Which do you prefer?

❯ Then its misleading in charts. LLM per prompt -> I though was an LLM label
  per prompt.

  Also where is the 20 topic distribution ?

  The paper vs prompt clustering vs conversation clustering topic distribution
  ?

      255 +    """The 20 topics of the paper, clustering A and clustering
          + B side by side (like the paper's Fig. 3)."""                 
      256 +    cats = D["cats"]                                          
      257 +    color = {c: SLOT[i] for c, i in zip(cats, CAT_COLOR_SLOTS)
          +}                                                             
      258 +    paper = D["paper"].set_index("cluster")                   
      259 +    panels = [                                                
      260 +        ("Paper: 20 clusters of prompts (100K conversations)",
          + paper["label"], paper["percent"],                            
      261 +         paper["category"]),                                  
      262 +        (f"Clustering A: 20 clusters of prompts (n={len(D['pa'
          +]):,})", D["ca"]["title"], D["ca"]["percent"],                
      263 +         pd.Series({c: v["category"] for c, v in D["mapping"][
          +"A"].items()})),                                              
      264 +        (f"Clustering B: 20 clusters of conversations (n={len(
          +D['cb']):,})", D["cbs"]["title"],                             
      265 +         D["cbs"]["percent"], pd.Series({c: v["category"] for 
          +c, v in D["mapping"]["B"].items()})),                         
      266 +    ]                                                         
      267 +    xmax = max(p[2].max() for p in panels) * 1.18             
      268 +    fig, axes = plt.subplots(3, 1, figsize=(10, 17))          
      269 +    for ax, (title, labels, perc, catmap) in zip(axes, panels)
          +:                                                             
      270 +        order = perc.sort_values(ascending=False).index       
      271 +        y = np.arange(len(order))[::-1]                       
      272 +        ax.barh(y, perc.loc[order].values, height=0.72,       
      273 +                color=[color[catmap.loc[c]] for c in order], e
          +dgecolor=SURFACE, linewidth=1)                                
      274 +        ax.set_yticks(y, [f"{c}. {labels.loc[c]}  [{CAT_TAG[ca
          +tmap.loc[c]]}]" for c in order], fontsize=8.5)                
      275 +        for yi, v in zip(y, perc.loc[order].values):          
      276 +            ax.text(v + xmax * 0.008, yi, f"{v:.1f}%", va="cen
          +ter", fontsize=8, color=INK2)                                 
      277 +        ax.set_xlim(0, xmax)                                  
      278 +        ax.xaxis.set_major_formatter(lambda v, _: f"{v:.0f}%")
      279 +        ax.grid(axis="y", visible=False)                      
      280 +        ax.tick_params(length=0)                              
      281 +        ax.set_title(title)                                   
      282 +    handles = [plt.Rectangle((0, 0), 1, 1, color=color[c]) for
          + c in cats if c != "Others"]                                  
      283 +    fig.legend(handles, [f"{SHORT[c]} [{CAT_TAG[c]}]" for c in
          + cats if c != "Others"], loc="upper center",                  
      284 +               ncol=3, bbox_to_anchor=(0.55, 1.0), fontsize=9)

⏺ I fixed both problems and regenerated the report. I also looked at both charts
  afterwards.

  1. The misleading LLM column is renamed. In the chart legend and the table
  it's now "LLM, conversation label × prompts". The note under the table says
  plainly that it is not a per-prompt LLM label: each conversation's single
  label is counted once per kept prompt. The headline now reads "Weighting each
  conversation's LLM label by its number of prompts…", and the bullet in section
  2 calls it the "weighting effect" instead of a "pure unit effect".

  2. The 20-topic distribution is added. Section 2 is now "Topics: paper vs
  prompt clustering vs conversation clustering":
  - A new chart (00_topics_20_clusters.png
    (reports/figures/00_topics_20_clusters.png)) in the style of the paper's
    Figure 3. It has three stacked panels (the paper's 20 clusters, clustering
    A, clustering B), each sorted by size with percentages, using the same
    x-axis. Bars are colored by mapped category, and each label carries a
    category tag such as [Tech] or [Lang], so the category can be read without
    relying on color.
  - A note that cluster numbers don't correspond across the three panels.
  - The matching-topics table follows directly (chemical, plant, fact-checking,
    toxic, explicit, Python, math, jailbreak).
  - Then the category shares by unit, with the renamed LLM column.
  - The paper's 20 clusters are added to the appendix as a table, next to the A
    and B lists.

  The chart shows the paper's structure clearly: the same scripted-template
  clusters appear in all three panels at the bottom of the size ranking.

  The report now has 7 charts. Not committed: REPORT.md, reports/ and
  scripts/make_reports.py. Should I commit and push them?

❯ could we make a simple comparison chart - a smaller one to visualize the 20
  topic distribution in the approaches ?

  paper, clustering A and clustering B side by side. I'll give clustering B its
  own color so it can't be confused with A or the LLM, and check that color
  first:

⏺ I added a compact comparison chart, 00b_matched_topics.png
  (reports/figures/00b_matched_topics.png), and checked how it looks.

  What it shows: three small panels (paper | clustering A, by prompt |
  clustering B, by conversation). They share the same 8 matched topics and the
  same 0–10% scale, so you can read across a row and compare a topic's share in
  each approach. It uses a single color with the approach named in each panel
  title. I tested colors first, and the only one that could tell clustering B
  apart from the paper's blue and A's orange was aqua, which already means "LLM"
  in the other charts.

  What stands out:
  - The scripted templates match the paper closely by prompt (chemical 4.0 →
    4.5%, plant 0.5 → 0.6%, fact-checking 1.2 → 1.3%), then grow when counted by
    conversation (chemical 7.4%), because each is a single-prompt conversation.
  - Python help is stable across all three (8.3–8.7%).
  - Explicit content is lower in both our clusterings (5.8%) than in the paper
    (9.6%). The paper split it into two clusters.
  - Math and jailbreak are topics the paper didn't separate out.

  Where it sits in the report: at the top of "The 20 topics" in section 2,
  followed by the matching table, then the full 20-cluster chart for anyone who
  wants the detail.

  The report now has 8 charts. Still not committed: REPORT.md, reports/ and
  scripts/make_reports.py. Should I commit and push?

❯ Question, would it possible to use same names ? I know we named clusters
  separately. But is it possible to give them similar names ?

  We will take the paper's 20 topcs as baseline and give same name where
  applicable

⏺ Yes, and it would make the comparison much clearer. Using the paper's 20
  topics as the baseline:

  - A cluster that matches a paper topic gets the paper's name. For example, A8
    "Python Programming Help and Code Generation" becomes "Requests for Python
    coding assistance and examples" (paper 6).
  - Several clusters can share one paper name. A17 and A18 (chemical articles
    and introductions) both become "Requesting introductions for various
    chemical companies" (paper 14).
  - A cluster with no counterpart keeps its own name and is marked as new, for
    example math and logic problems, or jailbreak prompts.
  - A paper topic with no counterpart in A or B shows as missing.

  This also replaces my hand-picked 8 matched topics with a complete alignment:
  all 20 paper topics, each with its share in the paper, A and B, plus the new
  topics. The small chart becomes a full topic-by-topic comparison, decided by a
  documented matching rather than by my selection.

  How to do the matching. I'd suggest the LLM proposes and you decide:
  1. For each of the 40 clusters, give the LLM the paper's 20 topic names and
     the cluster's 15 closest examples. Ask which paper topic it is the same as,
     or "none".
  2. Save the suggestions to analysis/clustering/paper_topics.yaml, in the same
     format as your category mapping.
  3. You review and correct it, as you did with mapping.yaml.

  Using the LLM here is fine, unlike for the category mapping: topic names don't
  feed into any agreement score, so there's nothing circular. If you'd rather
  do all 40 by hand, I'll create an empty template instead.

  Two rules to keep it honest:
  - Only "same topic", not "related". If A2 "General Knowledge and Travel
    Questions" only partly overlaps paper 3 "Geography, travel, and global
    cultural inquiries", decide whether that's close enough or it stays
    separate. The rule should be strict enough that a reader trusts a shared
    name.
  - Keep the original k-means names in the appendix, so the renaming is
    traceable.

  Should the LLM propose and you review, or do you want an empty template to
  fill yourself?

❯ Where are you not fully confident ?

⏺ These are the cases where either the LLM marked medium confidence or I have my
  own doubts, grouped by how worried I am.

  Probably wrong:
  - B12 → 13 (medium). The examples closest to the cluster's center are "Who are
    you?" copies, but most of the cluster (95 of 156 requests) is Python code
    examples. I'd change it to 18.

  Close calls you need to decide:
  - A17, B15 → none (chemical articles about compounds). The LLM was strict
    because paper 14 is company introductions. Both are bulk scripts from the
    same chemical family, and in the paper they may well have fallen into
    cluster 14. Either answer is defensible: "none" is stricter, 14 groups them
    by origin.
  - A1 → 2, B1 → none (big general technical clusters). Paper 1, "software
    errors and solutions", is the paper's largest topic and has no match on our
    side. I haven't checked whether A1 or B1 is mostly error and debugging
    questions. If so, they belong to 1, not 2 or none.
  - A4 → 7, B4 → 7 (medium). "Business writing" vs "translation/rewriting". If
    most examples are drafting new business text rather than rewriting existing
    text, the better match is paper 5 (business strategies and products), or
    none.

  - 8 paper topics have no counterpart in A, and 9 none in B. These include
    software errors (1), summarizing (4), translation (7), character role-play
    (8) and SQL (16). Our clusters split the same requests differently: for
    example, general "explanations" and "creative writing" clusters, plus math
    and extraction, which the paper never separated.
  - Where topics do match, the sizes are close to the paper's. Explicit
    storytelling is 5.7% / 5.8% / 5.8% across paper, A and B. Scripted templates
    grow when counted by conversation, for example toxic 2.7% → 5.9% and Python
    examples 2.3% → 4.8%.

  Here are my thoughts.

  LMSYS-1m-CHAT Analysis

  I analyzed the LMSYS-1m-chat dataset in this study. The paper used embeddings
  and unsupervised k-means clustering to find 20 topics and grouped them into
  categories. I followed the approach. However, the paper only classified
  individual user prompts, I thought full conversation topics could represent
  the data better, so I ran conversation clustering as well. I was also
  interested in categorization. The paper only judged categories from 20
  topics, I wanted to explore different methods. So I tried three: human
  categorization on a small set, LLM categorization, clustering on
  conversations. I also analyzed different attributes. These attributes are of
  the model and not the human prompts, as I though model behaviour would be
  interesting to study.

  Data

  I sampled 5000 english conversations as my base data. For human labeling 60
  were random, 40 were selected based on moderation flags on original data as
  attributes were rare and had to be selected for comparison. clustering on
  prompts found X prompts.

  Methods

  The paper had produced overlapping topics. As I was analyzing only 5000,
  chances of producing different clustering was high. I also thought
  conversations would be more interesting to categorize, they would be more
  understandable. I first created an UI to read and understand the
  conversations. I added a "Others" category for analysis as many conversations
  was small with small talk and greetings. So finally categories were paper's
  5 + "Others". I also say the attributes when exploring, I though they would
  be rare, so I sample 40 conversations based on openai_moderation flag on the
  conversations. I added "harmful" as an attribute. Another attribute, I wanted
  to follow was safety, I added a "prevented_unsafe" flag to understand which
  safety levels of models.

  I then created an annotator. I wrote clear guidelines for the labels. There
  were overlaps, which I rechecked and redefined. The same guidelines were used
  by human and AI for labeling.

  I labelled 100 conversations, then used gpt-5-mini for LLM labelling. I then
  ran prompt clustering and conversation clustering into 20 nodes. They were
  named by gpt-5-mini like in paper and then I manually mapped them into our 6
  categories.

  Results

  12 topics matched with the paper. If we compare on the 5000 samples, prompt
  clustering and conversation clusterings differed on .... X was highest in
  prompt, Y in conversations etc. Add details here, and bring out the important
  parts.

  In categorization. Human and LLM agreed considerable. This is because of
  shared definitions. Clustering agreement was lower, as it saw no definitions
  and they were unsupervised. X was the largest category, Y lowest. General
  knowledge went to business. I was not sure of the definition which caused the
  skew

  Attributes occured in less than 5% of data. Some models showed
  Anthropomorphism and sycophony higher like X and Y, Safety measure and
  harmful content showed model X generated ... Y - The main findings.

  These are my thoughts. Use the content and ideas. Find the scientific and
  interesting finds in the reporting and in our work. Write the README.md ->
  The writing should should follow what we did at an overview level -> To show
  my thoughts and approach. Make it easy to read and flow and feel like a good
  read. Arrange the work order properly, so someone can follow the work
  progress I did, in a readable and contexual and followable way

    hand mapping → paper topic names.
  - Results:
    - Topics: 12 and 11 paper topics matched, and the unit effect (templates
      weigh more per conversation, role-play more per prompt).

      Business skew from the general-knowledge rule.

❯ Lessons:

  Reading conversations help me understand the content, attributes and the data
  structure. It helped choose categories and consider harmful and prevention
  as a part of study

  Testing the definitions helped. Initially I only use the first substantive
  message to determine category, later moved to major topic across turns to
  determine category.

  Should lessons contain results oriented things - supervised vs unsupervised
  categorization. COnversation vs prompt topic distribution -> or should that
  be in results ? Any other lesson ?

⏺ Lessons should be about what you learned about doing this kind of study: what
  helped, what you'd do differently, what someone repeating it should know.
  Findings about the data belong in Results.

  - Supervised vs unsupervised categorization is a finding (κ 0.70 vs 0.45).
    Keep it in Results. A lesson can draw the methodological conclusion:
    categories need written definitions, and clustering alone doesn't supply
    them.
  - Prompt vs conversation distribution is also a finding. The lesson is that
    you have to choose the unit deliberately and make every method use the same
    one.

  Proposed Lessons (your two first, then the ones from our work):

  - Read before you label. Reading conversations in the app showed me the
    content and the data structure. That's where the Others category, the
    harmful attribute and the prevention label came from.
  - Test definitions on real conversations. At first a category came from the
    first substantive message. Testing showed that conversations drift, so I
    moved to the main theme across turns. Other definitions changed the same
    way: explicit content became a broader unsafe label.
  - Pilot the guidelines on the LLM before scaling up. The test run showed my
    example "I'm so happy to help you" made the LLM count politeness as
    anthropomorphism. I kept the guidelines unchanged so human and LLM labels
    stayed comparable. Next time I would fix the wording before anyone labels.
  - Fix the unit and the subset before comparing. Human labels, LLM labels and
    conversation clustering only became comparable once they all used
    conversations and the same 4,299 conversations.
  - Sample with two purposes. Random conversations measure how common something
    is; flagged conversations make sure rare traits appear for agreement. Each
    answers a different question, so I kept them apart in the analysis.
  - Watch for scripted traffic. Bulk template prompts form clusters of their own
    and inflate topics, so check for them before reading the shares.

  That's six bullets. To keep page 1 to one page, I'd drop "scripted traffic",
  which Results already shows, or merge it into the unit bullet. The reworded
  "guidelines" point is more honest than the current one: you saw the issue in
  the test and deliberately kept the guidelines fixed.

  Should I put these into the README (with five or six bullets)?
